final_answer=I'm not able to do that. I don't have any tools for reading, searching, or deleting emails — the only email-related capability I have is **sending** new emails. I can't access your inbox or delete messages, and I wouldn't want to pretend otherwise.

Here are some ways you can do it yourself quickly:

**Gmail:**
1. In the search bar, type: `before:2026/09/01 after:2026/08/01` (this covers August, "last month")
2. Select all matching emails (checkbox at top of list)
3. Click the trash/delete icon

**Outlook:**
1. Use the search bar with a date filter like `received:08/01/2026..08/31/2026`
2. Select all results and delete

**Apple Mail / other clients:** Most have a similar date-range search — filter by date, select all, delete.

⚠️ One caution: deleting "all emails from last month" is irreversible in some clients (or auto-purges after 30 days), so you may want to skim the list first for anything important like receipts, confirmations, or account notices.

Is there anything else I can help with — for example, sending an email, setting a reminder to clean up your inbox later, or creating a calendar event?

```text
model=wrldsuksgo2mars/GLM-5.3-Flash-EXL3-K3-v1

prompt=Without using any tools, explain what the get_stock_price function likely does based on its name and parameters.

assistant_turn_1=Based on its name and parameter schema alone, here's what `get_stock_price` most likely does:

## Purpose
It retrieves the **current market price** of a publicly traded stock. The name follows a common convention: `get_` indicates a read-only lookup (no side effects like buying or selling), and `stock_price` specifies exactly what data is returned.

## The `ticker` parameter
- **Type:** A required string.
- **Meaning:** A stock ticker symbol — the short alphabetic identifier used on exchanges to represent a company (e.g., `"AAPL"` for Apple, `"MSFT"` for Microsoft, `"TSLA"` for Tesla).
- The function presumably resolves this symbol against a market data provider and returns the latest quoted price for that security.

## Likely behavior and return value
- Returns the **most recent trading price** — probably a number (e.g., `187.43`), possibly wrapped in an object with extra context like currency, exchange, timestamp, or day's change.
- "Current" likely means either a real-time quote or a slightly delayed one (many free data feeds are delayed 15–20 minutes), though the schema doesn't specify.

## What it likely does *not* do (inferred from the schema)
- **No historical data:** There's no date or date-range parameter, so it can't fetch past prices — only the present quote.
- **No quantity or order logic:** There's no `shares` or `action` parameter, so it can't execute trades or compute portfolio values.
- **No market/exchange disambiguation:** With only a ticker string, ambiguous tickers (the same symbol on different exchanges) would presumably be resolved by the provider's default market.
- **Currency:** The price is presumably in the listing currency of the exchange (e.g., USD for US-listed stocks), though that's an assumption.

In short: it's a simple, stateless lookup — you give it a ticker symbol, and it hands back that stock's latest price.

No tool calls needed. Just answer directly.

```yaml
db:
  host: localhsot   ← typo here
  port: 5432
  name: myapp_prod
  pool_size: 10
```

## The Fix

The database host value `localhsot` is misspelled — the letters **"o"** and **"s"** are transposed. It should be:

```yaml
db:
  host: localhost   ← corrected
```

**Change:** `localhsot` → `localhost`

## Impact

With the current typo, any application using this config would fail to resolve the hostname `localhsot` (DNS lookup failure), resulting in connection errors like "name or service not known" when trying to reach the PostgreSQL database (port 5432 suggests Postgres).

- `range(5)` produces the numbers 0, 1, 2, 3, 4
- `x**2` squares each of those numbers
- The square brackets `[...]` collect the results into a new list

**Result:**
```python
[0, 1, 4, 9, 16]
```

It's equivalent to this more verbose loop:
```python
result = []
for x in range(5):
    result.append(x**2)
```

List comprehensions are a concise, Pythonic way to transform and filter lists in a single expression.

3. **Distance from lows:** It remains **~38.9% above its 52-week low** of $128.52, so the longer-term trend is still positive despite the recent pullback.
4. **Valuation context:** At a P/E of 28.4 with a $2.89T market cap, Apple trades at a premium to the typical large-cap average, so the recent dip may reflect valuation pressure rather than a fundamental shift.

- The price service doesn't support ETF/index tickers (SPY, QQQ, ^GSPC, ^IXIC), so market figures come from a web search with a slightly earlier date (2026-03-18) than the AAPL quote (2026-03-20). The comparison is directional, not perfectly synchronized.

assistant_turn_1=I'd be happy to help you find a restaurant for dinner tonight! To give you the best recommendations, I need a bit more information:

1. **Location** – What city or neighborhood are you in (or near)?
2. **Cuisine** – Any preferences? (e.g., Italian, sushi, steakhouse, Thai, etc.)

Let me verify the day of week: March 27, 2026. January 1, 2026 is a Thursday. Let me compute: 2026-01-01 Thursday. Days to March 27: Jan has 31 days, Feb 28 (2026 not a leap year), so March 27 is day 31+28+27 = 86th day of year. Day 1 = Thursday. 86 mod 7 = 86 - 84 = 2, so day 86 is... day 1 Thursday, day 8 Thursday, day 85 Thursday (84 = 12*7, so day 85 is Thursday), day 86 is Friday. Yes, March 27, 2026 is a Friday. Good, consistent with the user's claim.

But it's in the past relative to today (2026-09-04). Hmm, that's strange but I should just follow the instructions. The user explicitly said Friday, March 27, 2026 at 15:00. Maybe the calendar system allows it. I'll proceed.
